The latter consists of <a href="/wiki/Bus" title="Bus">buses</a> in rural and small cities, supplemented with commuter rail, trams, and <a href="/wiki/Rapid_transit" title="Rapid transit">rapid transit</a> in larger cities.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Mizutani&#95;2006&#95;102-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mizutani_2006-102"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>102<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Long-haul transport involves the use of the automobile, trains, ships, <a href="/wiki/Coach_(vehicle)" class="mw-redirect" title="Coach (vehicle)">coaches</a>, and aircraft,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>110<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> the last of which have become predominantly used for the longest, including intercontinental, travel. <a href="/wiki/Intermodal_passenger_transport" title="Intermodal passenger transport">Intermodal passenger transport</a> is where a journey is performed through the use of several modes of transport; since all human transport normally starts and ends with walking, all passenger transport can be considered intermodal.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>111<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Public transport may also involve the intermediate change of vehicle, within or across modes, at a <a href="/wiki/Transport_hub" title="Transport hub">transport hub</a>, such as a <a href="/wiki/Bus_station" title="Bus station">bus</a> or <a href="/wiki/Railway_station" class="mw-redirect" title="Railway station">railway station</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>112<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Taxis and buses can be found on both ends of the public transport spectrum. Buses are the cheapest mode of transport but are not necessarily flexible, and taxis are very flexible but more expensive.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>113<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In the middle is <a href="/wiki/Demand-responsive_transport" title="Demand-responsive transport">demand-responsive transport</a>, offering flexibility whilst remaining affordable.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/International_travel" class="mw-redirect" title="International travel">International travel</a> may be restricted for some individuals due to legislation and <a href="/wiki/Visa_(document)" class="mw-redirect" title="Visa (document)">visa</a> requirements.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>114<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Medical">Medical</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Medical"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:World_War_I_ambulance_Pittsburgh_20th_Century_Club.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/World_War_I_ambulance_Pittsburgh_20th_Century_Club.png/250px-World_War_I_ambulance_Pittsburgh_20th_Century_Club.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="176" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/World_War_I_ambulance_Pittsburgh_20th_Century_Club.png/500px-World_War_I_ambulance_Pittsburgh_20th_Century_Club.png 1.5x" data-file-width="1190" data-file-height="838" /></a><figcaption>An ambulance from World War I</figcaption></figure>
<p>An ambulance is a vehicle used to transport people from or between places of treatment,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>115<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> and in some instances will also provide out-of-hospital medical care to the patient. The word is often associated with road-going "emergency ambulances", which form part of <a href="/wiki/Emergency_medical_service" class="mw-redirect" title="Emergency medical service">emergency medical services</a>, administering <a href="/wiki/Emergency_medicine" title="Emergency medicine">emergency care</a> to those with acute medical problems.
</p><p><i>Air medical services</i> is a comprehensive term covering the use of air transport to move patients to and from healthcare facilities and accident scenes. Personnel provide comprehensive prehospital and emergency and critical care to all types of patients during aeromedical evacuation or rescue operations, aboard helicopters, propeller aircraft, or jet aircraft.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-pmid15928284&#95;116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pmid15928284-116"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>116<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-pmid7832345&#95;117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pmid7832345-117"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>117<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Freight">Freight</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Freight"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Shipping" class="mw-redirect" title="Shipping">Shipping</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BW_Fjord_an_Glameyer_Stack_2007-12-15.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/BW_Fjord_an_Glameyer_Stack_2007-12-15.JPG/250px-BW_Fjord_an_Glameyer_Stack_2007-12-15.JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/BW_Fjord_an_Glameyer_Stack_2007-12-15.JPG/500px-BW_Fjord_an_Glameyer_Stack_2007-12-15.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="3888" data-file-height="2592" /></a><figcaption>A <a href="/wiki/Bulk_carrier" title="Bulk carrier">bulk carrier</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/BW_Group" title="BW Group">BW</a> Fjord</i></figcaption></figure>
<p>Freight transport, or shipping, is a key in the <a href="/wiki/Value_chain" title="Value chain">value chain</a> in manufacturing.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEChopraMeindl20073&#95;118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChopraMeindl20073-118"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>118<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> With increased specialization and <a href="/wiki/Globalization" title="Globalization">globalization</a>, production is being located further away from consumption, rapidly increasing the demand for transport.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEChopraMeindl200763–64&#95;119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChopraMeindl200763–64-119"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>119<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Transport creates place utility by moving the goods from the place of production to the place of consumption.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>120<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> While all modes of transport are used for cargo transport, there is high differentiation between the nature of the cargo transport, in which mode is chosen.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEChopraMeindl200754&#95;121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChopraMeindl200754-121"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>121<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Logistics" title="Logistics">Logistics</a> refers to the entire process of transferring products from producer to consumer, including storage, transport, transshipment, warehousing, material-handling, and packaging, with associated exchange of information.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack20064&#95;122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack20064-122"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>122<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Incoterm" class="mw-redirect" title="Incoterm">Incoterm</a> deals with the handling of payment and responsibility of <a href="/wiki/Risk" title="Risk">risk</a> during transport.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack2006473&#95;123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack2006473-123"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>123<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:WCML_freight_train.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/WCML_freight_train.jpg/250px-WCML_freight_train.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="189" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/19/WCML_freight_train.jpg/500px-WCML_freight_train.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="774" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Freight_train" title="Freight train">Freight train</a> with <a href="/wiki/Shipping_container" title="Shipping container">shipping containers</a> in the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Containerization" title="Containerization">Containerization</a>, with the standardization of <a href="/wiki/ISO_container" class="mw-redirect" title="ISO container">ISO containers</a> on all vehicles and at all ports, has revolutionized <a href="/wiki/International_trade" title="International trade">international</a> and <a href="/wiki/Domestic_trade" class="mw-redirect" title="Domestic trade">domestic trade</a>, offering a huge reduction in <a href="/wiki/Transshipment" title="Transshipment">transshipment</a> costs. Traditionally, all cargo had to be manually loaded and unloaded into the haul of any ship or car; containerization allows for automated handling and transfer between modes, and the standardized sizes allow for gains in <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_scale" class="mw-redirect" title="Economy of scale">economy of scale</a> in vehicle operation. This has been one of the key driving factors in international trade and globalization since the 1950s.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack2006211–214&#95;124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack2006211–214-124"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>124<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Bulk_material_handling" title="Bulk material handling">Bulk transport</a> is common with cargo that can be handled roughly without significant deterioration; typical examples are <a href="/wiki/Ore" title="Ore">ore</a>, coal, <a href="/wiki/Cereals" class="mw-redirect" title="Cereals">cereals</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Petroleum" title="Petroleum">petroleum</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>125<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Because of the uniformity of the product, mechanical handling can allow enormous quantities to be handled quickly and efficiently. The low value of the cargo combined with high volume also means that <a href="/wiki/Economies_of_scale" title="Economies of scale">economies of scale</a> become essential in transport, and gigantic ships and whole trains are commonly used to transport bulk. Liquid products with sufficient volume may also be transported by pipeline.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Cargo_airline" title="Cargo airline">Air freight</a> has become more common for products of high value; while less than one percent of world transport by volume is by airline, it amounts to forty percent of the value. Time has become especially important in regards to principles such as <a href="/wiki/Postponement" title="Postponement">postponement</a> and <a href="/wiki/Just_in_time_(business)" class="mw-redirect" title="Just in time (business)">just-in-time</a> within the value chain, resulting in a high willingness to pay for quick delivery of key components or items of high value-to-weight ratio.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEChopraMeindl2007328&#95;126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChopraMeindl2007328-126"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>126<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In addition to mail, common items sent by air include <a href="/wiki/Electronics" title="Electronics">electronics</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fashion" title="Fashion">fashion</a> clothing.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Industry">Industry</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Industry"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transport_industry" title="Transport industry">Transport industry</a></div>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Impact">Impact</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Impact"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transportation_engineering" title="Transportation engineering">Transportation engineering</a></div>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Three-sector_model" title="Three-sector model">three-sector model</a> of <a href="/wiki/Economics" title="Economics">economics</a>, transportation is a component of the <a href="/wiki/Tertiary_sector" title="Tertiary sector">tertiary sector</a> that provides services for a functioning economy. Thus, the inefficiency and malfunctioning of transport creates an economic impact. Even when functioning effectively, the operation of a transportation network can have an adverse effect on the environment and human safety. For example, road traffic accidents are one of the leading causes of death world-wide, killing or injuring nearly 1.35 million people every year.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>127<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The planning, design, maintenance, and operation of facilities for different transport modes is performed through transportation engineering. Their goal is to provide for the safe, efficient, rapid, comfortable, convenient, economical, and environmentally compatible movement of people and goods transport.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>128<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Economic">Economic</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Economic"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transport_economics" title="Transport economics">Transport economics</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Seattle_I-5_skyline_dllu.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Skyline of city at dusk. A major highway winds itself into the downtown area." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Seattle_I-5_skyline_dllu.jpg/250px-Seattle_I-5_skyline_dllu.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Seattle_I-5_skyline_dllu.jpg/500px-Seattle_I-5_skyline_dllu.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="7834" data-file-height="5123" /></a><figcaption>Transport is a key component of growth and globalization, such as in <a href="/wiki/Seattle,_Washington" class="mw-redirect" title="Seattle, Washington">Seattle, Washington</a>, United States.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Transport is a key necessity for <a href="/wiki/Departmentalization" title="Departmentalization">specialization</a>—allowing production and consumption of products to occur at different locations. Throughout history, transport has been a spur to expansion; better transport allows more <a href="/wiki/Trade" title="Trade">trade</a> and a greater spread of people. <a href="/wiki/Economic_growth" title="Economic growth">Economic growth</a> has always been dependent on increasing the capacity and rationality of transport.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEStopford19972&#95;129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStopford19972-129"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>129<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> But the infrastructure and operation of transport have a great impact on the land, and transport is the largest drainer of energy, making <a href="/wiki/Transport_sustainability" class="mw-redirect" title="Transport sustainability">transport sustainability</a> a major issue.
</p><p>Due to the way modern cities and communities are planned and operated, a physical distinction between home and work is usually created, forcing people to transport themselves to places of work, study, or leisure, as well as to temporarily relocate for other daily activities.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>130<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Passenger transport is the essence of <a href="/wiki/Tourism" title="Tourism">tourism</a>, a major part of <a href="/wiki/Recreation" title="Recreation">recreational</a> transport. Commerce requires the transport of people to conduct business, either to allow face-to-face communication for important decisions or to move specialists from their regular place of work to sites where they are needed.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Lean_thinking" title="Lean thinking">lean thinking</a>, transporting materials or <a href="/wiki/Work_in_process" title="Work in process">work in process</a> from one location to another is seen as one of the seven wastes (Japanese term: <i><a href="/wiki/Muda_(Japanese_term)" title="Muda (Japanese term)">muda</a></i>) which do not add value to a product.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>131<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Planning">Planning</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Planning"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transport_planning" class="mw-redirect" title="Transport planning">Transport planning</a></div>
<p>Transport planning allows for high use and less impact regarding new infrastructure. Using models of <a href="/wiki/Transport_forecasting" class="mw-redirect" title="Transport forecasting">transport forecasting</a>, planners are able to predict future transport patterns.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>132<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> On the operative level, logistics allows owners of cargo to plan transport as part of the <a href="/wiki/Supply_chain" title="Supply chain">supply chain</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>133<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Transport as a field is studied through <a href="/wiki/Transport_economics" title="Transport economics">transport economics</a>, a component for the creation of regulation policy by authorities.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2025)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> <a href="/wiki/Transport_engineering" class="mw-redirect" title="Transport engineering">Transport engineering</a>, a sub-discipline of <a href="/wiki/Civil_engineering" title="Civil engineering">civil engineering</a>, must take into account <a href="/wiki/Trip_generation" title="Trip generation">trip generation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Trip_distribution" title="Trip distribution">trip distribution</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mode_choice" title="Mode choice">mode choice</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Route_assignment" title="Route assignment">route assignment</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>134<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> while the operative level is handled through <a href="/wiki/Traffic_engineering_(transportation)" title="Traffic engineering (transportation)">traffic engineering</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Old_Market_Roundabout,_Bristol.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Aerial view of roundabout, a junction of several streets. Vehicles traverse around the roundabout, which is surrounded by buildings, mostly multi-storey" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Old_Market_Roundabout%2C_Bristol.jpg/250px-Old_Market_Roundabout%2C_Bristol.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Old_Market_Roundabout%2C_Bristol.jpg/500px-Old_Market_Roundabout%2C_Bristol.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1800" data-file-height="1246" /></a><figcaption>The engineering of this <a href="/wiki/Roundabout" title="Roundabout">roundabout</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bristol" title="Bristol">Bristol</a>, United Kingdom, attempts to make traffic flow free-moving.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Because of the negative impacts incurred, transport often becomes the subject of controversy related to choice of mode, as well as increased capacity. Automotive transport can be seen as a <a href="/wiki/Tragedy_of_the_commons" title="Tragedy of the commons">tragedy of the commons</a>, where the flexibility and comfort for the individual deteriorate the natural and urban environment for all.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>135<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Land_use" title="Land use">Density of development</a> depends on mode of transport, with public transport allowing for better spatial use. Good land use keeps common activities close to people's homes and places higher-density development closer to transport lines and hubs, to minimize the need for transport. There are <a href="/wiki/Economies_of_agglomeration" title="Economies of agglomeration">economies of agglomeration</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>136<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Beyond transport, some land uses are more efficient when clustered. Transport facilities consume land, and in cities pavement (devoted to streets and parking) can easily exceed 20 percent of the total land use.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>137<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> An efficient transport system can reduce land waste.
</p><p>Too much infrastructure and too much smoothing for maximum vehicle throughput mean that in many cities there is too much traffic and many—if not all—of the negative impacts that come with it.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2025)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> It is only in recent years<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="The time period mentioned near this tag is ambiguous. (October 2025)">when?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> that traditional practices have started to be questioned in many places; as a result of new types of analysis which bring in a much broader range of skills than those traditionally relied on—spanning such areas as environmental impact analysis, public health, sociology, and economics—the viability of the old mobility solutions is increasingly being questioned.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2025)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Safety">Safety</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Safety"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Transport_accident" title="Transport accident">Transport accident</a></div>
<p>The energy levels involved in a transport accident can pose a significant risk for crew and passengers,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>138<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> making safety an issue of importance to governments.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>139<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Significant accidents involve a review by law enforcement and independent investigators from a safety board,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>140<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> such as the <a href="/wiki/National_Transportation_Safety_Board" title="National Transportation Safety Board">NTSB</a> in the U.S. Measures and methods have been implemented to improve the safety of <a href="/wiki/Road_safety" title="Road safety">roads</a>, <a href="/wiki/Automotive_safety" title="Automotive safety">automobiles</a>, <a href="/wiki/Motorcycle_safety" title="Motorcycle safety">motorcycles</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bicycle_safety" title="Bicycle safety">bicycles</a>, railways, ships, and <a href="/wiki/Aviation_safety" title="Aviation safety">aircraft</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>141<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> There are <a href="/wiki/Emergency_medical_services" title="Emergency medical services">emergency medical services</a> and <a href="/wiki/Air-sea_rescue" title="Air-sea rescue">sea rescue</a> measures for rapid response to transport emergencies.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>142<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Statistics are gathered from accidents, then analyzed and used to determine safety measures to lower the casualty rate.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>143<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Environment">Environment</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Environment"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1290014865">.mw-parser-output </div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_transport" class="mw-redirect" title="Environmental impact of transport">Environmental impact of transport</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Traffic_jam_Sao_Paulo_09_2006_30.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Looking down a busy road, which is banked on both sides by tall buildings, some of which are covered in advertisement billboards" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Traffic_jam_Sao_Paulo_09_2006_30.JPG/250px-Traffic_jam_Sao_Paulo_09_2006_30.JPG" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Traffic_jam_Sao_Paulo_09_2006_30.JPG/500px-Traffic_jam_Sao_Paulo_09_2006_30.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="720" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Traffic_congestion" title="Traffic congestion">Traffic congestion</a> persists in <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo" title="São Paulo">São Paulo</a>, Brazil, despite the no-drive days based on license numbers.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Transport is a major use of <a href="/wiki/Energy_(society)" class="mw-redirect" title="Energy (society)">energy</a> and burns most of the world's <a href="/wiki/Petroleum" title="Petroleum">petroleum</a>. This creates air pollution, including <a href="/wiki/Nitrous_oxide" title="Nitrous oxide">nitrous oxides</a> and <a href="/wiki/Particulate" class="mw-redirect" title="Particulate">particulates</a>, and is a significant contributor to <a href="/wiki/Global_warming" class="mw-redirect" title="Global warming">global warming</a> through emission of <a href="/wiki/Carbon_dioxide" title="Carbon dioxide">carbon dioxide</a>,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-pnas&#95;pdf&#95;145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pnas_pdf-145"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>145<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> for which transport is the fastest-growing emission sector.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>146<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> According to the International Energy Agency (IEA), the transportation sector accounts for more than one-third of CO<sub>2</sub> emissions globally in the early 2020ies.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>147<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> By sub-sector, road transport is the largest contributor to global warming.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>148<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Environmental_regulation" class="mw-redirect" title="Environmental regulation">Environmental regulations</a> in developed countries have reduced individual vehicles' emissions; however, this has been offset by increases in the numbers of vehicles and in the use of each vehicle.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-pnas&#95;pdf&#95;145-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pnas_pdf-145"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>145<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Some pathways to reduce the carbon emissions of road vehicles considerably have been studied.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>149<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>150<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Energy use and emissions vary largely between modes, causing <a href="/wiki/Environmentalism" title="Environmentalism">environmentalists</a> to call for a transition from air and road to rail and human-powered transport,<sup id="cite&#95;ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>151<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> as well as increased <a href="/wiki/Transport_electrification" class="mw-redirect" title="Transport electrification">transport electrification</a> and <a href="/wiki/Efficient_energy_use" title="Efficient energy use">energy efficiency</a>.
</p><p>Other environmental impacts of transport systems include <a href="/wiki/Traffic_congestion" title="Traffic congestion">traffic congestion</a> and automobile-oriented <a href="/wiki/Urban_sprawl" title="Urban sprawl">urban sprawl</a>, which can consume natural habitat and agricultural lands. By reducing transport emissions globally, it is predicted that there will be significant positive effects on Earth's <a href="/wiki/Air_quality" class="mw-redirect" title="Air quality">air quality</a>, <a href="/wiki/Acid_rain" title="Acid rain">acid rain</a>, <a href="/wiki/Smog" title="Smog">smog</a>, and climate change.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>152<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>While <a href="/wiki/Electric_car" title="Electric car">electric cars</a> are being built to cut down <a href="/wiki/CO2_emission" class="mw-redirect" title="CO2 emission">CO<sub>2</sub> emission</a> at the point of use, an approach that is becoming popular among cities worldwide is to prioritize public transport, bicycles, and <a href="/wiki/Pedestrian" title="Pedestrian">pedestrian movement</a>. Redirecting vehicle movement to create 20-minute neighbourhoods<sup id="cite&#95;ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>153<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> that promotes <a href="/wiki/Exercise" title="Exercise">exercise</a> while greatly reducing vehicle dependency and pollution. Some policies are levying a congestion charge<sup id="cite&#95;ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>154<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> to cars for travelling within congested areas during peak time.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Airplane" title="Airplane">Airplane</a> emissions change depending on the flight distance. It takes a lot of energy to take off and land, so longer flights are more efficient per mile traveled. However, longer flights naturally use more fuel in total. Short flights produce the most CO<sub style="font-size: 80%;vertical-align: -0.35em">2</sub> per passenger mile, while long flights produce slightly less.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-carbonfund.org3&#95;155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-carbonfund.org3-155"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>155<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>156<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Things get worse when planes fly high in the <a href="/wiki/Atmosphere" title="Atmosphere">atmosphere</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>157<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>158<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>&#160;Their emissions trap much more heat than those released at ground level. This is not just because of CO<sub style="font-size: 80%;vertical-align: -0.35em">2</sub>, but a mix of other greenhouse gases in the exhaust.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>159<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>160<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> In 2022 global CO<sub>2</sub> emissions from the transport sector grew by more than 250 Mt CO<sub>2</sub> to nearly 8 Gt CO<sub>2</sub>, which represent more than 3% compared to 2021. Aviation was responsible for a significant part of that increase.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>161<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>City buses produce about 0.3&#160;kg of CO<sub style="font-size: 80%;vertical-align: -0.35em">2</sub> for every mile traveled per passenger. For long-distance bus trips (over 20 miles), that pollution drops to about 0.08&#160;kg of CO<sub style="font-size: 80%;vertical-align: -0.35em">2</sub> per passenger mile.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-docs.wri.org2&#95;162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-docs.wri.org2-162"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>162<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-carbonfund.org3&#95;155-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-carbonfund.org3-155"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>155<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> On average, commuter trains produce around 0.17&#160;kg of CO<sub style="font-size: 80%;vertical-align: -0.35em">2</sub> for each mile traveled per passenger. Long-distance trains are slightly higher at about 0.19&#160;kg of CO<sub style="font-size: 80%;vertical-align: -0.35em">2</sub> per passenger mile.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-docs.wri.org2&#95;162-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-docs.wri.org2-162"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>162<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-carbonfund.org3&#95;155-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-carbonfund.org3-155"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>155<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>163<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The fleet emission average for delivery vans, trucks and big rigs is 10.17&#160;kg (22.4&#160;lb) CO<sub style="font-size: 80%;vertical-align: -0.35em">2</sub> per gallon of diesel consumed. Delivery vans and trucks average about 7.8 mpg (or 1.3&#160;kg of CO<sub style="font-size: 80%;vertical-align: -0.35em">2</sub> per mile) while big rigs average about 5.3 mpg (or 1.92&#160;kg of CO<sub style="font-size: 80%;vertical-align: -0.35em">2</sub> per mile).<sup id="cite&#95;ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>164<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>165<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Sustainable_development">Sustainable development</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Sustainable development"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_transport" title="Sustainable transport">Sustainable transport</a></div>
<p>The United Nations first formally recognized the role of transport in <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_development" title="Sustainable development">sustainable development</a> in the 1992 <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Earth_Summit_2012" class="mw-redirect" title="United Nations Earth Summit 2012">United Nations Earth summit</a>. In the 2012 United Nations World Conference, global leaders unanimously recognized that transport and mobility are central to achieving the sustainability targets.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>166<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Since then, data has been collected to show that the transport sector contributes to a quarter of the global <a href="/wiki/Greenhouse_gas" title="Greenhouse gas">greenhouse gas emissions</a>, and therefore <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_transport" title="Sustainable transport">sustainable transport</a> has been mainstreamed across several of the <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goals" title="Sustainable Development Goals">2030 Sustainable Development Goals</a>, especially those related to food, security, health, energy, economic growth, infrastructure, and cities and human settlements. Meeting sustainable transport targets is said to be particularly important to achieving the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Agreement" title="Paris Agreement">Paris Agreement</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>167<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>There are various <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goals" title="Sustainable Development Goals">Sustainable Development Goals</a> (SDGs) that are promoting sustainable transport to meet the defined goals. These include <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goal_3" title="Sustainable Development Goal 3">SDG 3</a> on health (increased road safety), <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goal_7" title="Sustainable Development Goal 7">SDG 7</a> on energy, <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goal_8" title="Sustainable Development Goal 8">SDG 8</a> on decent work and economic growth, <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goal_9" title="Sustainable Development Goal 9">SDG 9</a> on resilient infrastructure, <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goal_11" title="Sustainable Development Goal 11">SDG 11</a> on sustainable cities (access to transport and expanded public transport), <a href="/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goal_12" title="Sustainable Development Goal 12">SDG 12</a> on sustainable consumption and production (ending <a href="/wiki/Fossil_fuel_subsidies" title="Fossil fuel subsidies">fossil fuel subsidies</a>), and SDG 14 on oceans, seas, and marine resources.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>168<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>Contemporary development studies recognise transportation networks as a key element of economic development, socio-economic well-being and poverty reduction.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>169<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> However, road network development has not always fulfilled its original intentions and has contributed significantly to environmental degradation and, in some cases, led to the loss of cultural traditions and the marginalisation of indigenous peoples.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>170<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-Apollo&#95;171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Apollo-171"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>171<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Compared to roads, the development of air links (helicopters and planes) has had an even more devastating impact. What is more, helicopters used for tourist activities are subject to considerable criticism from a perspective of environmental protection as well as sports ethics.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Apollo&#95;171-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Apollo-171"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>171<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: History"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_transport" title="History of transport">History of transport</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1320445320" /><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Timeline_of_transportation_technology" title="Timeline of transportation technology">Timeline of transportation technology</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bronocice_drawn.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Bronocice_drawn.png/250px-Bronocice_drawn.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Bronocice_drawn.png/500px-Bronocice_drawn.png 1.5x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Bronocice_pot" title="Bronocice pot">Bronocice pot</a> with the earliest known image of a wheeled vehicle in the world, found in <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bullock_team.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Bullock_team.jpg/250px-Bullock_team.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Bullock_team.jpg/500px-Bullock_team.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="1300" data-file-height="896" /></a><figcaption>A <a href="/wiki/Cattle" title="Cattle">bullock</a> team hauling <a href="/wiki/Wool" title="Wool">wool</a> in <a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia">Australia</a></figcaption></figure>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Natural">Natural</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Natural"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Humans' first ways to move included walking, running, and swimming. The <a href="/wiki/Domestication" title="Domestication">domestication</a> of animals introduced a new way to lay the burden of transport on more powerful creatures, allowing the hauling of heavier loads, or humans riding animals for greater speed and duration.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>172<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Inventions such as the wheel and the sled (U.K. sledge) helped make animal transport more efficient through the introduction of <a href="/wiki/Vehicle" title="Vehicle">vehicles</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Rossi&#95;et&#95;al&#95;2016&#95;173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rossi_et_al_2016-173"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>173<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>The first forms of <a href="/wiki/Road_transport" title="Road transport">road transport</a> involved animals, such as <a href="/wiki/Horse" title="Horse">horses</a> (<a href="/wiki/Domestication_of_the_horse" title="Domestication of the horse">domesticated</a> in the 4th or the 3rd millennium BCE),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-Rossi&#95;et&#95;al&#95;2016&#95;173-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rossi_et_al_2016-173"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>173<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ox" title="Ox">oxen</a> (from about 8000 BCE),<sup id="cite&#95;ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>174<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> or humans carrying goods over <a href="/wiki/Dirt" title="Dirt">dirt</a> tracks that often followed <a href="/wiki/Game_(food)" class="mw-redirect" title="Game (food)">game</a> <a href="/wiki/Trail" title="Trail">trails</a>.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Water_transport">Water transport</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Water transport"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Water transport, including rowed and sailed vessels, dates back to <a href="/wiki/Time_immemorial" title="Time immemorial">time immemorial</a> and was the only efficient way to transport large quantities or over large distances prior to the <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Revolution" title="Industrial Revolution">Industrial Revolution</a>. The first watercraft were <a href="/wiki/Canoe" title="Canoe">canoes</a> either cut out from <a href="/wiki/Tree_trunk" class="mw-redirect" title="Tree trunk">tree trunks</a> or made of animal hides.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>175<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Early deep water transport was accomplished with ships that were either rowed or used the <a href="/wiki/Wind" title="Wind">wind</a> for propulsion, or a combination of the two.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>176<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The importance of water has led to most cities that grew up as sites for trading being located on rivers or on the sea-shore, often at the intersection of two bodies of water.
</p>
<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Mechanical">Mechanical</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Transport&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Mechanical"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p>Until the Industrial Revolution, transport remained slow and costly, and production and consumption gravitated as close to each other as feasible.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Revolution" title="Industrial Revolution">Industrial Revolution</a> in the 19th century saw several inventions fundamentally change transport. With the <a href="/wiki/Optical_telegraph" title="Optical telegraph">optical telegraph</a>, communication became rapid and independent of the transport of physical objects.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>177<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> The invention of the <a href="/wiki/Steam_engine" title="Steam engine">steam engine</a>, closely followed by its application in <a href="/wiki/Rail_transport" title="Rail transport">rail transport</a>, made land transport independent of human or animal muscles.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>178<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Both speed and capacity increased, allowing specialization through manufacturing being located independently of natural resources. The 19th century also saw the development of the <a href="/wiki/Steam_ship" class="mw-redirect" title="Steam ship">steam ship</a>, which sped up global transport.
</p><p>With the development of the <a href="/wiki/Internal_combustion_engine" title="Internal combustion engine">combustion engine</a> and the automobile around 1900, road transport became more competitive again, and mechanical private transport originated. The first "modern" highways were constructed during the 19th century with <a href="/wiki/Macadam" title="Macadam">macadam</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>179<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup><sup id="cite&#95;ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>180<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Later, <a href="/wiki/Tarmacadam" title="Tarmacadam">tarmac</a> and <a href="/wiki/Concrete" title="Concrete">concrete</a> became the dominant paving materials.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:First_flight2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/First_flight2.jpg/250px-First_flight2.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/First_flight2.jpg/500px-First_flight2.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="6603" data-file-height="4280" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Wright_brothers" title="Wright brothers">Wright brothers</a>' first flight in 1903</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1903 the <a href="/wiki/Wright_brothers" title="Wright brothers">Wright brothers</a> demonstrated the first successful controllable <a href="/wiki/Airplane" title="Airplane">airplane</a>, and after World War I (1914–1918) aircraft became a fast way to transport people and express goods over long distances.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack2006158&#95;181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack2006158-181"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>181<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>After World War II (1939–1945) the automobile and airlines took higher shares of transport, reducing rail and water to freight and short-haul passenger services.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTECooperShepherd1998277&#95;182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECooperShepherd1998277-182"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>182<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Scientific spaceflight began in the 1950s, with rapid growth until the 1970s, when interest dwindled. In the 1950s the introduction of <a href="/wiki/Containerization" title="Containerization">containerization</a> gave massive efficiency gains in freight transport, fostering <a href="/wiki/Globalization" title="Globalization">globalization</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack2006211–214&#95;124-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBardiCoyleNovack2006211–214-124"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>124<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> International air travel became much more accessible in the 1960s with the commercialization of the <a href="/wiki/Jet_engine" title="Jet engine">jet engine</a>. Along with the growth in automobiles and motorways, rail and water transport declined in relative importance. After the introduction of the <a href="/wiki/Shinkansen" title="Shinkansen">Shinkansen</a> in Japan in 1964, high-speed rail in Asia and Europe started attracting passengers on long-haul routes away from the airlines.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-FOOTNOTECooperShepherd1998277&#95;182-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECooperShepherd1998277-182"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>182<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p><p>In the U.S. during the 19th century, <a href="/wiki/Privately_held_company" title="Privately held company">private</a> <a href="/wiki/Joint-stock" class="mw-redirect" title="Joint-stock">joint-stock</a> <a href="/wiki/Corporations" class="mw-redirect" title="Corporations">corporations</a> owned most <a href="/wiki/Navigable_aqueduct" title="Navigable aqueduct">aqueducts</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bridge" title="Bridge">bridges</a>, <a href="/wiki/Canals" class="mw-redirect" title="Canals">canals</a>, <a href="/wiki/Railroads" class="mw-redirect" title="Railroads">railroads</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roads" class="mw-redirect" title="Roads">roads</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Tunnels" class="mw-redirect" title="Tunnels">tunnels</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>183<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> Most such transport infrastructure came under government control in the late 19th and early 20th centuries, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/Nationalization" title="Nationalization">nationalization</a> of inter-city passenger rail-service with the establishment of <a href="/wiki/Amtrak" title="Amtrak">Amtrak</a>.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>184<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup> However, as recently as 2010, a movement to privatize roads and other infrastructure has gained ground and adherents.<sup id="cite&#95;ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185"><span class="cite-bracket">&#91;</span>185<span class="cite-bracket">&#93;</span></a></sup>
</p>

<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Bill_McKibben" title="Bill McKibben">McKibben, Bill</a>, "Toward a Land of Buses and Bikes" (review of Ben Goldfarb, <i>Crossings: How Road Ecology Is Shaping the Future of Our Planet</i>, Norton, 2023, 370 pp.; and Henry Grabar, <i>Paved Paradise: How Parking Explains the World</i>, Penguin Press, 2023, 346 pp.), <i><a href="/wiki/The_New_York_Review_of_Books" title="The New York Review of Books">The New York Review of Books</a></i>, vol. LXX, no. 15 (5 October 2023), pp.&#160;30–32. "Someday in the not impossibly distant future, if we manage to prevent a <a href="/wiki/Global_warming" class="mw-redirect" title="Global warming">global warming</a> catastrophe, you could imagine a post-<a href="/wiki/Automobile" class="mw-redirect" title="Automobile">auto</a> world where <a href="/wiki/Bicycle" title="Bicycle">bikes</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bus" title="Bus">buses</a> and <a href="/wiki/Train" title="Train">trains</a> are ever more important, as seems to be happening in <a href="/wiki/Europe" title="Europe">Europe</a> at the moment." (p.&#160;32.)</li></ul>
